a:T857,The paper has two technical threads. The first is regularization. The authors revisit the apparent advantage of diffusion-style masked training over autoregressive training in data-constrained settings and ask whether that advantage partly reflects under-regularized AR baselines. To isolate the effect, they apply the AR-tuned strong weight decay to diffusion language models as well, and find that dLLMs improve substantially under the same stronger regularization. Once both families are strongly regularized, their validation losses become much closer. This is an important control: it separates the effect of masking from the effect of insufficient regularization.

They then introduce masked-input regularization, or MIR, as a minimal augmentation of standard autoregressive pretraining. The model keeps the usual next-token prediction loss on clean sequences, but also optimizes an auxiliary next-token prediction loss on randomly masked versions of the same inputs. The architecture and inference procedure remain unchanged; only training is modified. The extra cost is additional training compute, which the paper treats as acceptable because the target regime is compute-rich but data-limited. MIR is therefore positioned as a data-efficiency technique rather than a compute-efficiency technique.

The second thread is scaling-law modeling. The authors compare several candidate forms and show that additive Chinchilla-style laws fit poorly in this regime. The issue is structural: if loss decomposes additively into a model term plus a data term, then the loss difference between two data budgets should not depend on model size. Their experiments contradict that prediction. To address this, they propose SoftQ, a five-parameter scaling law with a soft bottleneck coupling between model size and data size, motivated by a skill-learning view of scaling. The idea is that when unique data is scarce, extra model capacity cannot be fully utilized, and conversely the value of more data depends on whether the model is large enough to absorb additional skills. A coupled law is therefore more appropriate than an additive one.b:T561,The empirical study spans models from 72M to 1.4B parameters and uses DCLM and Stack-V2 in data-constrained pretraining setups. The regularization results have two main layers. First, diffusion language models also benefit substantially from the same strong weight decay tuned for AR models, which shows that heavy weight decay is a general requirement of this regime rather than an AR-specific patch. Second, MIR consistently improves validation loss even on top of strongly regularized AR baselines, indicating that random masking contributes complementary regularization rather than merely substituting for weight decay. At 1.4B parameters, the gains carry over downstream, including +10.2 points on BoolQ and +2.2 points on SciQ.

For scaling, the study expands to five model sizes and four unique-data budgets, then fits multiple scaling-law families. SoftQ outperforms additive Chinchilla, Quanta, and Muennighoff-style laws both in-sample and out-of-sample. The same ranking also holds on an independent dataset from Kim et al. (2026b), which strengthens the claim that the result is not an artifact of one corpus. Using SoftQ as the baseline law, the paper estimates that MIR is worth roughly 1.3x more unique training data at the 200M-400M token budgets. That framing is especially useful because it converts a training modification into an equivalent data multiplier.c:T4d4,For a quick but meaningful read, start with Figure 1. Panel (a) shows whether MIR adds value beyond strong weight decay across scales; panel (b) shows why the authors claim that Chinchilla is structurally wrong in this regime rather than simply underfit. Then read the problem setup carefully: the paper is explicitly about fixed unique data with multiple epochs, not the standard abundant-data single-pass setting. In the method section, focus on the MIR objective and how the clean-input and masked-input losses are combined, including the masking scheme and training mixture. In the experiments, prioritize three checks: whether strong weight decay matters equally for AR and dLLM training, whether MIR remains effective across model sizes and data budgets, and how large the out-of-sample prediction gap is between SoftQ and the additive baselines. A 30-45 minute skim can cover the introduction, method, Figure 1, the main result tables, and the scaling-law plots. For deeper reading, two questions are worth pursuing: whether MIR helps mainly through corruption-induced invariance or through smoother optimization dynamics, and whether the SoftQ coupling transfers to larger models, longer training runs, or mixed-quality corpora.d:T687,The method, GRASP, stands for Geometry-aware Residual Alignment for Scalable Pretraining Data Attribution. It starts from a one-step smoothness lower bound on utility improvement under weighted subset interventions. This leads to a surrogate with two parts: a linear train-target relevance term and a quadratic train-train geometric penalty. The linear term measures how aligned a training example is with the evaluation target. The quadratic term captures interactions inside the selected subset, which is the key departure from additive attribution. When expanded, it naturally penalizes redundant examples and rewards complementary coverage.

For scalability, the paper avoids exact influence-function style computation. Instead, it uses the outer-product structure of last-layer gradients in causal language models. For a token, the local gradient can be written as hidden state h times next-token residual ξ = p - y. Sequence-level gradients are sums of such token contributions. GRASP then compresses these high-dimensional objects into low-dimensional feature sketches, which are used to approximate both train-target alignment and train-train geometry. This is a clear engineering trade-off: less exactness, but reusable artifacts and fast subset scoring at pretraining scale.

Another strong point is the evaluation protocol. The paper fixes component weights and retain/delete modes on development environments before testing, instead of tuning per task. That matters because many attribution methods look stronger than they are once hidden oracle tuning is removed. Here the intended object is a reusable surrogate, not a benchmark-specific fitting procedure.e:T4dc,The main evaluation target is counterfactual fidelity under subset retraining, measured by Linear Datamodeling Score (LDS), a rank correlation between predicted subset utility and realized utility after retraining. The paper reports that GRASP decisively outperforms scalable baselines such as influence-style methods, TRAK, DataInf, and LESS in subset-level prediction. The headline numbers in the paper are that GRASP more than doubles task-level LDS rank correlation over prior scalable baselines, reduces artifact construction time by nearly an order of magnitude, and can score 100,000 candidate subsets in about 5 seconds.

The experimental design emphasizes two failure modes of additive attribution: duplicate-heavy or highly clustered data, where additive methods overvalue redundant examples, and complementary slices, where interaction-aware scoring should better capture joint utility. The ablations focus on whether the quadratic term is necessary, how sketch dimensionality affects the accuracy-speed trade-off, and whether the method is stable for both retain and delete interventions. The main takeaway is that most of the gain comes from modeling interactions, not merely from swapping in a better train-target relevance proxy.f:T452,Start with the introduction and problem setup to understand why the paper moves from pointwise attribution to subset-level utility prediction. Then go straight to the derivation from the smoothness lower bound to the final 'linear term plus quadratic term' surrogate. That is the conceptual center of the paper. After that, inspect the pipeline figure and the implementation details for residual alignment, geometric penalty, and feature sketching.

In the experiments, prioritize the subset retraining protocol, the LDS definition, the main comparison tables, and the analyses on duplicates versus complementary coverage. Pay attention to whether hyperparameters are truly fixed before test evaluation, whether baselines are converted fairly into subset predictors, and whether the interaction term helps consistently across settings. A useful skim should take around 30 to 45 minutes. If reading more deeply, the most interesting questions are the gap between the theoretical lower bound and the practical surrogate, and how robust the sketch approximation is for larger models and richer feature layers.10:T46f,LightningLM 0.1V is a recurrence-backbone language-model family grown in four stages: a 1.78B dense seed, then 5B and 9B MoE stages, and finally a 118.67B sparse MoE. The largest model uses 460 routed experts with top-12 routing, and activates 5.93B parameters per token path, roughly five percent of the stored parameters. Each stage is initialized from the previous trained model. The paper emphasizes preserving the learned interfaces during growth, rather than treating expansion as a mostly random reinitialization. Two supporting design choices matter for the memory budget. The model uses BrahmicTokenizer-131K, a 131,072-token byte-level BPE tokenizer, and replaces a conventional learned embedding table with a Kronecker input embedding. A standard lookup table at this vocabulary and width would require about 537M trainable parameters. The Kronecker projection uses about 33.55M trainable parameters and constructs embeddings from frozen byte and position bases through a trained projection. It also avoids materializing a reconstruction table that would otherwise occupy about 2.15GB per GPU, or about 17GB across the node.11:T85f,方法核心是 OPDLM 和其中的 On-Policy Distillation。学生模型来自一个 ARLM checkpoint，但把 causal attention 改成 bidirectional attention，使其可以对被 mask 的任意位置做条件建模。教师模型则保留原始冻结 ARLM。关键难点在于，AR teacher 和 DLM student 的状态空间不同：teacher 习惯在完整左前缀上做 next-token prediction，student 则在部分已知、部分 mask 的序列状态上做去噪。作者的处理方式不是把 teacher 直接搬到随机 mask 状态上，而是让 student 先按自己的 diffusion 解码策略 rollout 出一条反向去噪轨迹，从中采样一个部分去噪状态 x_t，再对其中仍被 mask 的位置做蒸馏。

蒸馏目标的构造很讲究。对每个 masked 位置 i，student 输出该位置的 token 分布；teacher 则读取终态序列 x_0 中该位置之前的 causal prefix，给出 AR 条件分布，作为该位置的监督信号。也就是说，teacher 提供的是“如果按 AR 方式生成到这里，应该偏向哪些 token”，student 学的是“在当前部分去噪状态下，对这些尚未恢复的位置给出与 teacher 一致的分布”。这样做同时解决两件事：一是知识保留，因为监督直接来自原 AR 模型的 logits，而不是只靠 diffusion NELBO 重新学；二是 train-test mismatch，因为训练样本来自 student 自己的 on-policy rollout，而不是离线随机 mask。

论文还加入 rollout-length curriculum。直觉上，训练初期学生模型还不稳定，如果一开始就在很长的去噪轨迹上做 on-policy 学习，状态分布会太差，蒸馏信号也更噪。于是作者逐步增加 rollout 长度，让学生先在较短、较容易的轨迹上对齐，再扩展到更接近真实推理的状态。这个设计和序列决策里的 curriculum / DAGGER 风格很接近，但这里的监督源是冻结 AR teacher。另一个值得注意的点是，作者把 OPDLM 明确定位成 general-purpose DLM 和 task-specific DLM 两种用法：既可以把通用 AR 基座转成通用 DLM，也可以在特定任务上做更便宜的转换。12:T802,The central idea is OPDLM, trained with On-Policy Distillation. The student starts from an ARLM checkpoint whose causal attention is replaced with bidirectional attention, enabling denoising over arbitrary masked positions. The teacher is the original frozen ARLM. The main challenge is that the teacher and student do not share the same state space: the teacher predicts the next token from a causal prefix, while the student denoises partially observed sequences.

The paper resolves this by letting the student generate its own reverse denoising trajectories. At each training step, the student rolls out a trajectory under its own diffusion decoding policy, and one partially denoised state x_t is sampled from that trajectory. Distillation is then applied only to the still-masked positions in x_t. For each masked position i, the student predicts a token distribution conditioned on the partially denoised sequence, while the teacher provides target logits conditioned on the causal prefix of the terminal sequence x_0 up to position i. In effect, the teacher says what an AR model would prefer at that position, and the student learns to match that preference on the states it will actually encounter during inference.

This design addresses both mismatches at once. Knowledge retention improves because supervision comes directly from the original AR teacher rather than from relearning everything through a diffusion NELBO. Train–test mismatch is reduced because training states are on-policy student states, not uniformly random masks. The paper also introduces a rollout-length curriculum: early in training, the student is supervised on shorter and easier trajectories, and the rollout horizon is gradually increased as the model stabilizes. That choice is important for optimization, since long on-policy trajectories from an immature student would otherwise create noisy and low-quality supervision. The framework is presented both as a general-purpose AR-to-DLM conversion method and as a cheaper task-specific DLM adaptation method.13:T597,The most informative results are about efficiency and the necessity of on-policy training. On efficiency, the headline number is striking: OPDLM-8B uses only 0.066B training tokens and 4.2 × 10^18 FLOPs, while establishing a new known Pareto frontier on AIME-24. Relative to prior DLM or AR-to-DLM conversion systems such as LLaDA-8B, Dream-7B, SDAR-8B, and Fast-dLLM-v2-7B, the required training tokens are reduced by roughly 15× to 7,000×. That is not a marginal saving; it turns conversion into something much closer to heavy post-training than continued pretraining.

On task performance, the paper evaluates across general-purpose and reasoning-heavy settings, including math and coding, and also studies task-specific conversion. The emphasis is not on claiming the single best absolute score everywhere, but on maintaining strong performance under dramatically lower training cost. The key ablation compares on-policy and off-policy distillation, and on-policy consistently wins, directly supporting the train–inference mismatch argument. Additional appendices study smaller-scale settings, the effect of teacher size, multi-seed evaluation on GPQA-Diamond and AIME variants, and comparisons against standard supervised fine-tuning. The inference section also shows that the converted DLM can exploit multi-token decoding, so the method is not merely changing the training loss while keeping AR-like inference behavior.14:T527,Read it in the order of problem setup, method, efficiency evidence, and ablations. Start with the introduction and Figure 1 to understand that the paper is about two mismatches in AR-to-DLM conversion, not generic distillation. Then focus on Section 4. Sections 4.1 and 4.2 are the core: 4.1 explains why standard DLM training does not match inference-time states, and 4.2 defines how AR teacher supervision is projected onto those on-policy student states. Section 4.3 on rollout-length curriculum is also worth careful reading because it is likely important for stability. In the experiments, prioritize Sections 5.2 and 5.3: the former tests whether the method works as a general-purpose DLM conversion recipe, and the latter shows why on-policy distillation matters. Then read 5.4 for the practical value of multi-token decoding, and 5.5 for the task-specific setting. In the appendix, A.2, A.4, and A.5 are especially useful for understanding teacher-size dependence, variance across seeds, and the boundary between this method and ordinary SFT. A good question to keep in mind while reading is whether an AR teacher can cap the student’s ceiling. The paper’s answer is pragmatic: first preserve the AR model’s competence under the new bidirectional denoising regime, then benefit from the DLM decoding form.15:T5af,WAV v1 is a lightweight extension of Block AttnRes. In Block AttnRes, each block stores only a summary C_b, the sum of all residual updates inside the block. WAV v1 keeps this summary and adds two zero-sum detail bases. The first is a phase basis, D_phase, defined by the signed difference between attention and MLP updates, capturing whether the block is more attention-dominant or MLP-dominant. The second is a split basis, D_split, defined by the signed difference between the first-half and second-half updates in the block, capturing coarse early-versus-late directional change. This gives each block not only a total displacement but also two cheap descriptors of its internal trajectory shape. The stabilization choices are conservative. Detail sources are initialized with a negative routing bias, beta_D = -2.0, so the model starts close to Block AttnRes and only uses details when useful. Detail tensors are RMS-matched to the corresponding block summary and clipped by a maximum scale factor rho, preventing them from dominating merely due to larger raw scale. The final prediction mixer does not read detail sources by default; they are used only in intermediate routing. The attention, MLP, embeddings, and output head remain unchanged. Computationally, the number of routing sources grows from about O(N) block summaries to about O(3N) block-basis sources, still block-level rather than layer-level, and the parameter overhead is negligible.16:T5cb,FAIR-Calib has two stages. Stage I is teacher probing. A full-precision teacher is used to estimate a position-aware prior that combines two signals: whether a position is at the write frontier, and how reliable the teacher is at that masked-stage position and timestep. Intuitively, positions that are both frontier-exposed and intrinsically unstable should receive more protection during calibration. The authors argue that this prior is mechanism-driven and therefore transfers reasonably across calibration corpora. Stage II is off-policy, layer-wise calibration. Instead of running expensive end-to-end diffusion rollouts with the quantized model, the method uses a teacher-forcing surrogate and aligns hidden states layer by layer under teacher trajectories. The loss is a reweighted hidden-state MSE, where the weights come from Stage I. This means calibration no longer treats all positions and timesteps equally; it explicitly prioritizes fragile frontier states. The paper also motivates an additive time-by-position weighting scheme and explains why weighted hidden-state MSE is a reasonable surrogate for preserving commit stability under mild assumptions. At the quantizer level, the implementation follows a FlatQuant-style learnable affine reparameterization to flatten weight and activation distributions before symmetric uniform quantization. The novelty is therefore not a new quantizer, but a dLLM-specific calibration target and an efficient off-policy procedure.17:T4d8,The experimental story is built around both end-task quality and mechanism-level diagnostics. The main setting is W4A4 PTQ on diffusion LLMs such as Dream and LLaDA. FAIR-Calib consistently outperforms naive transfers of AR-LLM PTQ baselines across multiple benchmarks, with the largest benefit on harder reasoning tasks where dLLMs are more brittle under perturbation. More importantly, the paper reports improvements on three internal metrics: fewer teacher-forced commit-step flips, reduced post-commit mismatch including both mean-disagree and more severe never-agree cases, and flatter probability-MSE traces showing weaker stepwise error amplification after a false commit. The provided excerpt does not include the full numeric tables, but the full text indicates several meaningful ablations: removing frontier-aware weighting, removing reliability gating, or dropping the off-policy weighted calibration each hurts performance. This suggests the gains come from the full two-stage design rather than a single heuristic. Another notable result is the cross-corpus transferability of the Stage I prior, which supports the claim that the estimated weights capture a decoding-mechanism property rather than dataset-specific statistics.18:T7da,The technical route has three main pieces. First, the paper reformulates attention approximation in terms of softmax KDE. This is natural because attention weights are normalized exponential kernels between queries and keys. Once written this way, the problem can be attacked using tools from coresets, polynomial approximation, high-dimensional geometry, and communication complexity.

Second, the analysis is split by regime. The table of contents makes clear that the authors treat a high-temperature regime and a low-temperature regime separately. Intuitively, when temperature is high, softmax is smoother and more amenable to low-degree approximation; when temperature is low, the kernel becomes sharper, local geometry matters more, and compression becomes harder. The paper therefore avoids a single coarse statement and instead derives regime-specific upper and lower bounds.

Third, the upper and lower bounds use different toolchains. On the upper-bound side, the paper employs multivariate polynomial methods, Hermite polynomials, KDE coresets, and a merge-and-reduce style conversion from offline compression to streaming compression. In the low-temperature regime, the contents indicate a more delicate pipeline: partition the dataset into pseudo-random and low-radius components, recenter and rescale them, build coresets for each part, then turn the offline compressor into a streaming algorithm. This suggests that the main technical novelty lies in decomposing the geometry of the instance before compressing it.

On the lower-bound side, the paper uses information-theoretic and communication-complexity arguments to show that some memory requirements are inherent rather than artifacts of loose analysis. The final section transfers the softmax-KDE results back to attention, yielding formal bounds for streaming attention itself.

Relative to prior work by Haris et al. and Kochetkova et al., the contribution here is not a new framework but a tighter characterization of the same problem.19:T424,This is a theory paper, so there are no standard model-training experiments or benchmark tables. The main outputs are theorems: upper and lower bounds in different regimes, and the extent to which the gap between them is reduced. From the structure, Theorem 2.1 and Theorem 2.2 appear to correspond to the high-temperature and low-temperature settings, and Section 7 translates the softmax-KDE statements into attention guarantees.

If one reads the theorems as the equivalent of empirical results, the key quantities to inspect are the memory complexity as a function of approximation error epsilon, ambient dimension, and the temperature or radius parameter r, together with whether upper and lower bounds match up to logarithmic or lower-order factors. The provided excerpt contains the table of contents rather than the theorem statements, so it would be unsafe to invent exact formulas. What is clear is that the paper sharpens the theory by treating the two regimes separately and by using a finer geometric decomposition in the upper-bound construction.1a:T427,Do not start from the proofs. Begin with the introduction and the statement of results to identify exactly which gaps from earlier work are being closed. Then read the technical overview, focusing on why the high-temperature and low-temperature regimes require different arguments.

If your interest is systems or inference optimization, Section 7, “From Softmax KDE to Attention,” is the most relevant part because it contains the translation back to KV-cache compression. If you care more about the theory itself, the upper-bound construction in Section 6 is likely the most informative, especially the partition into pseudo-random and low-radius components, the recenter-and-rescale step, and the offline-to-streaming conversion. The lower bounds are useful for understanding which engineering goals may simply be too optimistic.

A skim should take about 25–40 minutes. A full proof read will take much longer. It is helpful to keep the earlier papers by Haris et al. and Kochetkova et al. nearby and compare exactly where the new bounds become tighter.1b:T836,OffQ has a simple three-stage logic: identify the outlier direction, concentrate outlier energy into very few channels, then convert that energy into offsets that can be absorbed by the quantizer zero-point.

First, it identifies a low-dimensional outlier subspace using a tailored top-1 PCA procedure. The motivation comes from recent observations that high-norm activation outliers share similar directions across layers, token identities, and sequence positions. Instead of expensive full PCA or learned transformations, OffQ extracts the dominant outlier direction with a lightweight top-1 procedure.

Second, it applies an orthonormal rotation so that large-magnitude activations are concentrated into one or a few channels. This is related to rotation-based methods such as QuaRot and SpinQuant, but the goal is different. Those methods mainly try to spread energy and make the distribution more Gaussian-like. OffQ explicitly tries to gather structured outlier mass into a minimal set of channels so that it can be removed cleanly afterward.

Third, it performs group-wise offset absorption. Channels are reordered into groups, each assigned one outlier channel. A specialized Hadamard rotation is then applied within each group to transform the outlier energy into a shared offset. That offset is absorbed into the zero-point of standard low-bit quantization. Intuitively, instead of forcing the quantizer to represent a very large outlier channel directly, the method rewrites its effect as a group-level baseline shift. The remaining values then have much smaller variance and can be quantized more accurately.

The design choices are important. It keeps uniform quantization rather than switching to non-uniform codebooks, which helps hardware compatibility. It avoids mixed precision, so execution remains homogeneous. It also avoids backpropagation-based rotation learning, reducing calibration cost and implementation burden. The main assumption is that outliers are indeed low-dimensional and directionally stable; if that structure breaks, top-1 concentration may become less effective.1c:T645,The paper focuses on the difficult W4A4KV4 setting, where weights, activations, and KV cache are all quantized to 4 bits. This is a much stronger test than weight-only quantization and is the right place to evaluate whether outlier handling is genuinely effective.

From the provided full-content excerpt, the authors report consistent improvements in both perplexity and downstream accuracy across multiple LLM architectures and benchmarks, compared with a range of strong baselines, while preserving the simplicity of end-to-end uniform low-bit inference. The excerpt does not include the exact numeric tables, but the intended comparisons are clear: standard PTQ without strong outlier handling, rotation-based baselines such as QuaRot and SpinQuant, and more complex alternatives such as learned equivalent transforms or mixed-precision methods.

The most informative experiments are likely not only the headline metrics but also the distributional analyses: whether standard deviation, tail mass, and extreme-value concentration drop after top-1 PCA rotation and offset absorption; how sensitive the method is to group size and channel reordering; whether the gains are stable across layers and model families; and whether the runtime overhead of the extra transforms is small enough that the uniform 4-bit execution path still delivers practical speedups.

If the full paper includes comparisons against mixed-precision or learned-rotation methods, the key question is whether OffQ reaches similar perplexity with a simpler kernel path, or clearly better accuracy at comparable execution complexity.1d:T488,For a skim, start with the introduction and separate the three existing solution families: stronger transformations, mixed precision, and non-uniform quantization. Then read the method figure. The main idea is not just another rotation trick; it is to turn outlier channels into offsets that the quantizer zero-point can absorb.

In the method section, focus on four details: how top-1 PCA is defined and estimated, why the rotation can concentrate outliers into one channel, how channel grouping and reordering are constructed, and how the Hadamard transform makes offset absorption compatible with a standard quantizer. If those pieces are mathematically clean, the method is likely practical.

In the experiments, prioritize the main comparison table, activation-statistics plots, and latency or throughput results. If time is limited, first compare against QuaRot, SpinQuant, and mixed-precision baselines, then check the ablations. Estimated reading time is around 20 minutes. If you care about serving or low-cost model evaluation, it is worth reading the implementation details as well, because deployment simplicity is a major part of the contribution.1e:T85e,作者先用 Part 1 的带宽/拓扑基准建立心智模型：每个 Hopper 有本地 HBM 很快；每个 Hopper 到各自 Grace CPU 的 NVLink-C2C 很快；但两张 Hopper 之间不是常规 GPU P2P，跨 GPU 通信需要经由 CPU 侧 staging，代价高。基于这个模型，推理策略应尽量让热 decode 路径只流本地 HBM 权重，避免频繁跨 GPU。

在 vLLM 上跑 DeepSeek V4 Flash 时，作者对比了官方 checkpoint 与 Canada-Quant 的量化/混合精度 checkpoint，发现后者在该拓扑的 batch=1 单流 decode 明显更快。作者给出的机制解释不是“目录更小”，而是“热路径上哪些张量以什么格式被访问、触发了哪些 vLLM kernel 路径”：Canada-Quant 采用混合布局（路由专家权重 W4 packed、注意力投影 FP8、部分敏感张量保留 BF16/FP32），在单流 decode 下减少了活跃专家权重带宽压力，并可能更容易走到更合适的 vLLM kernel。

MTP 部分的核心是修复 checkpoint/运行时契约不一致：Canada-Quant 的 MTP block 里存在 BF16 未量化张量（如 mtp.0.*），而 vLLM 的 DeepSeek V4 NVIDIA O-projection 路径假设该投影有 FP8 scale 元数据（适用于全 FP8 路径）。结果是主模型能 load，但 MTP draft 启动失败。作者讨论两条路：
1）工件侧：把 MTP 张量也量化进 compressed-tensors 格式（更“干净”，但需要额外量化流程与可能的校准数据）；
2）运行时侧：在 vLLM 增加 BF16 fallback——检测 wo_a 是否存在 FP8 scale；有则走 FP8 kernel，无则用 BF16 做 inverse RoPE、reshape grouped 权重、grouped matmul，再接 wo_b。

此外作者还指出要让 MTP 端到端跑通，实际有三个相邻但独立的坑：artifact 元数据需匹配 vLLM 运行时模块名（不仅是 safetensors 名）；vLLM 构建 MTP e_proj/h_proj 时要正确 propagate prefix= 让 compressed-tensors 能匹配层名；以及 O-proj 执行路径要支持 BF16 fallback。另一个同类问题是 draft 侧 config 需要继承 target 侧的 hf_config_path，避免 target/draft 读取到不同 config 导致行为不一致。1f:T951,The post starts from a bandwidth/topology model established in Part 1: each Hopper has very fast local HBM; each Hopper has a fast NVLink-C2C link to its paired Grace CPU; but the two Hoppers do not form a normal GPU P2P pair, so cross-GPU traffic effectively stages through the CPU side and is much slower. The practical implication is simple: for batch=1 decode, you want the hot path to stream weights from local HBM and avoid designs that induce frequent cross-GPU transfers.

On top of that, the author benchmarks DeepSeek V4 Flash in vLLM using two artifacts: the official checkpoint and a Canada-Quant mixed-format checkpoint. The key observation is that the quantized artifact is much faster on this topology even though it is not smaller on disk. The proposed mechanism is “hot-path tensor traffic and kernel selection,” not directory size: Canada-Quant uses W4-packed routed expert weights, FP8 attention projections, and keeps some sensitive tensors in BF16/FP32. That can reduce active expert-weight bandwidth and steer vLLM into more favorable kernel paths for single-stream decode.

For MTP, the core issue is a checkpoint/runtime mismatch. The Canada-Quant artifact leaves MTP block tensors (e.g., mtp.0.*) in BF16/unquantized form, while vLLM’s DeepSeek V4 NVIDIA O-projection path expects FP8 scale metadata for wo_a (valid for a fully-FP8 pipeline). Result: the main model loads, but MTP draft initialization/execution fails.

Two fixes are discussed:
- Artifact-side: quantize MTP tensors into the same compressed-tensors format (cleaner, but requires an actual quantization pass and possibly calibration data).
- Runtime-side: add a BF16 fallback in vLLM’s O-proj op—check if wo_a has FP8 scale metadata; if yes, use the FP8 path; if not, run inverse RoPE in BF16, reshape grouped weights, do grouped matmul, then continue through wo_b.

The author also highlights three adjacent “contract” issues that must all be correct for stock end-to-end MTP: artifact metadata must match vLLM runtime module names (not just safetensors names); vLLM must propagate prefix= when constructing MTP e_proj/h_proj so compressed-tensors can match layer names; and the O-proj execution path needs the BF16 fallback for unquantized wo_a. A related pitfall is config inheritance: the draft path must inherit hf_config_path from the target path to avoid diverging configs.20:T40d,Its main contribution is evaluation and trend estimation, but it matters for pretraining research as well. First, it turns latent reasoning from a qualitative concern into a quantitative object by mapping performance onto a 50% task-completion time horizon. That is a more interpretable capability scale than isolated benchmark scores. Second, it introduces a complementary reasoning-token horizon, defined via the minimum number of reasoning tokens an anchor model needs to solve a task. This gives a bridge between explicit reasoning budget and latent reasoning ability. Third, the paper finds that no-CoT horizons are improving substantially, but still much more slowly than CoT-enabled full-affordance agentic horizons. That suggests explicit scratchpads, tool use, and externalized reasoning remain major capability multipliers. For pretraining researchers, this raises a direct question: which training objectives and data regimes encourage internal compression of reasoning, and which make reasoning more legible and controllable?21:T6a2,The methodology has three layers. First, benchmark construction: the authors assemble 43 benchmarks with more than 30,000 questions spanning math, coding, puzzles, causality, theory of mind, strategic reasoning, and multi-turn tool-use settings. The key constraint is that models are prevented from emitting reasoning tokens before the answer, so the setup aims to measure latent reasoning rather than explicit CoT. Second, capability scaling via task-completion horizons: following and extending prior horizon work, they estimate the human time required for tasks that a model completes with 50% success rate. To make this credible, they explicitly model uncertainty in human solve times using a mix of real human timings, collected baselines, and calibrated model-based estimates, then propagate uncertainty through hierarchical bootstrap over benchmarks, problems, model stochasticity, and time estimates. Third, they define a reasoning-token horizon using o3-mini as an anchor reasoning model: task difficulty is measured by the minimum number of reasoning tokens the anchor needs to solve the task within k attempts. This provides a model-native difficulty scale that complements the human-time scale. For trend analysis, they fit no-CoT horizon growth over release dates for 14 frontier models, using the Pareto frontier of capability versus release date for the main trend. They also fit horizon against architecture scale for 35 open-weight models, relating 50% TH to total parameters and layer count. One practical trade-off is that the datasets are not publicly released to avoid future training contamination, which limits reproducibility but is understandable for long-term measurement.22:T69f,Several numbers are central. First, across 14 frontier models from GPT-2 to GPT-5.5, the no-CoT 50% task horizon doubles every 373 days, with a 95% confidence interval of 167 to 691 days and R² of 0.84. GPT-5.5 reaches an estimated no-CoT horizon above 3 minutes. Second, the median extrapolation projects horizons above 7 minutes by 2028 and 25 minutes by 2030, though the paper is careful to note large uncertainty. Third, the reasoning-token horizon doubles every 437 days, with a 95% CI of 341 to 571 days. The mapping between token horizon and human-time horizon is sublinear rather than linear, implying that token requirements grow faster than human solve times as tasks get longer. Fourth, compared with Kwa et al.’s 182-day doubling for CoT-enabled full-affordance agentic tasks, the no-CoT trend is roughly twice as slow. That is an important empirical sign that explicit CoT and tool use still materially extend the time horizon of tasks models can handle. Fifth, on 35 open-weight models, doubling the 50% TH is estimated to require about 4.2× more total parameters or 1.3× more layers. That is only a rough scaling fit, but it hints that depth may matter more than raw parameter count for latent reasoning horizons. Two experimental design choices are especially strong: the benchmark suite is broad rather than math-only, and uncertainty from human-time estimation and model stochasticity is explicitly propagated instead of hidden behind a single trend line. The main caveats are that TH depends on human-time calibration, which can vary by task and strategy, and that “no CoT” prompting may not perfectly eliminate compressed forms of hidden or quasi-explicit reasoning.23:T5d2,This is worth a close read. Start with the introduction plus Figures 1 and 2 to lock in the two main metrics: no-CoT task horizon and reasoning-token horizon, and how they differ from prior CoT-enabled horizon estimates. Then go straight to the methodology for horizon estimation. Pay particular attention to how human solve times are estimated, how the 50% success point is fit, and how hierarchical bootstrap propagates uncertainty. Those details determine whether the trend line is trustworthy. After that, inspect the benchmark section: coverage of the 43 tasks, possible contamination risks, and whether the no-CoT constraint is implemented consistently across APIs and model families. In the experiments, focus on three groups of results: per-model horizon estimates with confidence intervals, the relationship between task horizon and token horizon, and the scaling fits on open-weight models versus parameter count and depth. The appendices matter a lot here, especially the measurement floor, the power-law relation between TH and token horizon, and the details of benchmark filtering and human-time calibration. If reading time is limited, prioritize the main-method section, Figures 1 and 2, the uncertainty model, and the appendices defining the horizon metrics. For pretraining readers, one extra angle is worth probing: which model families show larger gaps between no-CoT and CoT performance, since that may reflect different training-time dependence on explicit scratchpads.24:T7ca,The authors build on BigVGAN and convert it from a mel-spectrogram vocoder into a discrete-unit vocoder, then systematically vary the conditioning setup. Discrete units come from self-supervised speech representations followed by k-means clustering. Each 16 kHz audio segment contains 8320 samples and corresponds to 26 units; unit embeddings have dimension 128. The model can optionally take two kinds of side information.

1. Speaker conditioning: a 192-dimensional continuous speaker embedding from a pretrained ECAPA-TDNN, instead of a speaker lookup table tied to training identities. The reason is important: lookup tables memorize seen speakers and can entangle identity with language, while continuous d-vector-style embeddings are better suited for generalization and identity preservation.
2. Language conditioning: a learned language embedding, repeated over time and concatenated channel-wise with the unit embeddings and speaker embeddings before entering the generator.

The paper also adds a mel-based language identification classifier to reduce language interference. This shows that the goal is not only waveform fidelity but also keeping generated speech in the correct language manifold.

The technical contribution is less about a new loss or architecture and more about controlled factor analysis: varying cluster size, speaker conditioning, and language conditioning, then measuring their effects on intelligibility, speaker consistency, and unit-level structure. A key modeling takeaway is that discrete units are not naturally a clean “content-only” interface. In multilingual multi-speaker settings, the codebook carries phonetic, language, and speaker information at once. Instead of asking the generator to absorb all of that entanglement, the paper separates the problem into representation resolution and explicit conditional disentangling. That framing is more relevant to pretraining researchers than many system papers that treat the vocoder as a black box.25:T667,Experiments cover four Indian languages—Bengali, Hindi, Tamil, and Telugu—spanning Indo-Aryan and Dravidian families. Evaluation goes beyond WER and includes speaker similarity and unit-level metrics, which is a meaningful strength. The main findings are consistent and easy to interpret.

First, larger cluster sizes improve phonetic discriminability and usually improve intelligibility. This suggests that a larger discrete inventory helps separate content information rather than merely increasing memorization capacity.

Second, explicit speaker conditioning is critical for preventing identity collapse. Without speaker embeddings, multilingual multi-speaker training tends to produce speaker mixing, where generated speech drifts toward an average identity or gets biased by language-linked characteristics. Adding ECAPA-TDNN speaker embeddings substantially improves speaker similarity.

Third, language conditioning helps most at smaller cluster sizes. When the codebook is small, units compress phonological structure from different languages into a shared space, increasing cross-lingual interference; explicit language labels then help recover the correct language-specific characteristics. As cluster size grows, the units themselves provide stronger phonetic separation, so the marginal benefit of language embeddings decreases.

The paper is positioned as a systematic analysis rather than a single-number optimization paper. The provided text does not include the full result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact gains, but the qualitative conclusions are clear and tightly aligned with the experimental design.26:T4d8,The paper introduces PolyFact, a fully parallel multilingual factual multiple-choice QA dataset grounded in Wikidata truthy triples. It keeps 22 factual relations across geography, biography, creative works, organizations, and cultural relations, and covers 12 typologically diverse high- and low-resource languages. Distractors are sampled from co-occurring objects under the same property and matched by type and length. Questions are generated with Gemma-3-27B-IT and checked with a web-grounded GPT-4o judge plus manual review; the authors also provide a cleaner subset for ambiguous relations. The training comparison includes light CPT on TED2025-style parallel text, SFT with a classification-plus-consistency loss, and multilingual GRPO. In GRPO, each factual item is evaluated through 12 language-specific prompts, with G=8 grouped rollouts. The reward combines per-language correctness, invalid-answer handling, and an extra bonus when all languages are correct. The SFT baseline includes a cross-language distribution-matching term with stop-gradient so that the comparison is not simply RL versus no consistency pressure. The paper also applies LAHIS to inspect how fine-tuning changes language-specific attention-head behavior.27:T8a0,方法核心是 TRUST：Tool-calling decision Reward with Uncertainty-Separated post-Training。论文把 agent 每个决策点建模为四分类动作空间：Direct、Tool、Ask、Unable。状态由历史轨迹和可用工具集合组成，策略输出结构化响应。与已有做法只根据动作是否正确给奖励不同，TRUST 在 turn-level reward 里加入 uncertainty-aligned 项，把不确定度差距当成一个“排斥力”。

具体思路是：对当前状态，除了 ground-truth decision，还考虑负向或反事实决策；然后根据模型对这些候选动作的不确定度，构造一个 certainty margin。训练目标鼓励正确动作具有更低不确定度，同时让错误动作维持更高不确定度，而不是一并被 RL 推成高置信。这样做的意义在于，错误但低不确定的样本会收到更强惩罚，策略更新会被推离这些不可靠区域，探索信号也更健康。

从全文描述看，这里的 UQ 不是单独训练一个 uncertainty head，而是从策略分布中提取决策不确定度，再把它写进 reward。也就是说，它不是后处理校准，而是 reward shaping。相比 Ross et al.、Suri et al. 这类 decision-oriented RL，区别不在动作空间或环境建模，而在奖励函数显式编码了 calibration structure。

另一个设计点是把训练从单点决策扩展到多轮轨迹。作者没有给整条轨迹做重标注，而是只标关键 decision turns，用轻量标注把两层信号结合起来：trajectory-level reward 负责整体任务成功和工具执行质量，turn-level UQ-aligned reward 负责工具调用时机与动作适配性。这个分层设计比较务实，因为全轨迹精标成本高，而且很多 agent 失败确实集中在少数关键转折点。

从方法取舍上看，TRUST不是去改 inference-time verifier，也不是加一个外部 uncertainty model，而是尽量复用现有 RL post-training 流程，只改 reward 结构。这使它更像一个可插拔训练模块。代价则是效果依赖于不确定度估计是否稳定；如果 policy logits 本身噪声大，或者 sampled action space 太窄，certainty margin 的质量会受影响。28:T946,The method is TRUST: Tool-calling decision Reward with Uncertainty-Separated post-Training. Each decision point is modeled as a four-way action choice: Direct, Tool, Ask, or Unable. The state consists of the interaction history and the available tool set, and the policy outputs a structured response. The key change is in the reward. Instead of rewarding only whether the chosen action is correct, TRUST adds an uncertainty-aligned term that acts like a repulsive force against unreliable decisions.

The idea is to consider not only the ground-truth action for the current state, but also negative or counterfactual decisions. From the model’s uncertainty over these alternatives, the method constructs a certainty margin. Training encourages lower uncertainty on the correct decision while maintaining relatively higher uncertainty on incorrect ones. This matters because standard RL tends to increase confidence on rewarded samples without preserving the geometry of confidence across correct and incorrect actions. TRUST explicitly penalizes wrong-but-low-uncertainty cases, which should improve both calibration and exploration.

From the full-text description, uncertainty is extracted from the policy itself rather than from a separate uncertainty head. So this is reward shaping with uncertainty-aware signals, not post-hoc calibration. Relative to prior decision-oriented RL approaches, the novelty is not in the action space or environment, but in encoding calibration structure directly into the reward.

A second design choice is extending the setup from isolated decisions to multi-turn trajectories. Instead of fully relabeling every step, the authors annotate lightweight key decision turns. Training then combines two levels of supervision: trajectory-level rewards for overall task success and tool execution quality, and turn-level UQ-aligned rewards for whether tool use is timely and appropriate. This is a practical compromise, since many agent failures are concentrated at a few critical turns and full trajectory annotation is expensive.

The method is attractive because it can likely plug into existing RL post-training pipelines with minimal architectural changes. The main caveat is that its benefit depends on the quality of uncertainty estimation. If policy uncertainty is noisy or poorly identified, the certainty margin may become unstable.29:T557,The most informative empirical result is the failure analysis that motivates the paper. On Qwen3-4B-Thinking, standard decision-oriented RL increases the fraction of “wrong decision but low uncertainty” cases from 34.50% to 70.21%. That is a strong sign that RL can indeed collapse uncertainty separation while improving reward.

On benchmark performance, TRUST improves turn-level tool-calling accuracy by more than 11% on When2Call. When combined with trajectory-level tool-calling post-training, it also improves more complex multi-turn benchmarks: +6.33% on BFCL-V4 and +7.07% on ToolSandbox. The gains are reported to be larger in decision-heavy settings, including Multi-Turn and Irrelevance subsets on BFCL-V4, and scenarios with multiple user turns or distracting tools in ToolSandbox. That pattern is consistent with the method’s intended effect.

The most important evidence to inspect in the full paper is not just average benchmark gains, but whether the uncertainty structure is actually repaired. Useful tables and plots should include: calibration or uncertainty-separation statistics before and after RL, breakdowns of unsupported tool calls versus hallucinated direct answers, and ablations separating turn-level UQ reward from trajectory-level reward. The abstract does not provide all those details, so the main text and appendix matter here.2a:T46f,Start with the introduction and Figure 1. The paper stands or falls on one observation: standard RL improves correctness while collapsing uncertainty separation. If that analysis is convincing, the rest of the method becomes meaningful.

Then read the reward formulation carefully. Three details matter most: how uncertainty is computed, how the certainty margin is combined with correctness, and how negative or counterfactual actions are defined. Those choices determine whether the method can generalize beyond the specific benchmarks.

In the experiments, prioritize three pieces: 
1. plots comparing uncertainty distributions under standard RL versus TRUST;
2. the main results on When2Call, BFCL-V4, and ToolSandbox;
3. subset results for multi-turn and distraction-heavy scenarios.
A 30-40 minute read is enough to capture the main idea. For a deeper pass, inspect the ablations: removing the UQ term, using only turn-level reward, using only trajectory-level reward, and varying the uncertainty estimator. Those ablations tell you whether the gain comes from the uncertainty-aware design or simply from adding more supervision.2b:T8b9,方法叫 PTD-PO，Privileged Tutoring Distillation Policy Optimization。整体框架是在标准 RLVR，文中以 GRPO 为代表的 group-based policy optimization 之上，加一个针对失败轨迹的 tutoring distillation 分支。

核心思想不是让 teacher 看到标准输入加答案，而是给 frozen reference model 额外提供 answer-free hints。提示信息包括视觉 grounding cues 和高层 reasoning guidance，用来帮助 teacher 在不泄露最终答案的情况下，给出更合理的 token 分布。student 仍然只在原始、无答案上下文下生成。这样 teacher 和 student 处在信息不对称的条件下，属于 learning with privileged information 的变体。

为什么要这样设计，而不是直接 answer-conditioned self-distillation？作者的论点是，答案条件会让 teacher 分布过于尖锐，student 学到的是“朝答案收缩”的捷径，而不是如何恢复正确推理路径。PTD-PO 则试图只提供方向性纠偏，不提供终点本身，因此能在 failed rollout 上给出 dense signal，同时保留探索空间。

技术上还有一个关键点：teacher 和 student 的上下文不同，直接做全词表 KL/JSD 对齐既不稳定也费显存。为此作者提出 Top-K Jensen-Shannon Divergence with tail compensation。直观理解是，只对 teacher/student 分布中最重要的 top-k token 做精细对齐，其余长尾概率质量用补偿项近似，从而在保持分布对齐稳定性的同时降低内存开销。这个设计比单纯 top-k KL 更稳，因为 JSD 本身对分布错配更温和，而 tail compensation 避免把长尾完全截断。

训练流程上，PTD 模块主要作用于 RLVR 中的失败样本，把 outcome-level 的 0/1 或稀疏 reward 转成 token-level 学习信号。论文还强调它与不同 RLVR optimizer 兼容，说明蒸馏项是附加在 policy optimization 外层，而不是绑定某个特定算法。

方法的潜在限制也比较清楚：hint 的构造质量决定 teacher 是否真能提供“教方法不教答案”的信号；如果 hint 过强，仍可能形成隐式泄露；如果 hint 太弱，teacher 分布和 student 差别不大，dense supervision 的收益会下降。2c:T979,The method is PTD-PO: Privileged Tutoring Distillation Policy Optimization. It starts from a standard RLVR setup, instantiated with group-based policy optimization such as GRPO, and adds a tutoring distillation branch targeted at failed rollouts.

The key design is to avoid answer-conditioned teachers. Instead, a frozen reference model receives answer-free hints, including visual grounding cues and high-level reasoning guidance. These hints are meant to help the teacher infer a better corrective direction without revealing the final answer. The student policy, however, still operates under the original answer-free context. This creates an asymmetric teacher-student setup in the spirit of learning with privileged information.

Why not simply use answer-conditioned self-distillation? The paper argues that answer-revealing contexts make the teacher distribution too sharp and too deterministic. The student then learns to collapse toward the known answer rather than learning how to recover a valid reasoning path. PTD-PO tries to provide directional guidance instead of endpoint information, so failed rollouts can receive dense token-level supervision while preserving exploration.

A second technical contribution is the distillation objective. Because teacher and student are conditioned on different contexts, full-vocabulary KL or JSD alignment is both memory-heavy and potentially unstable. The paper proposes a Top-K Jensen-Shannon Divergence objective with tail compensation. Intuitively, it aligns the most important top-k probability mass explicitly while approximating the remaining tail mass with a compensation term. This reduces memory cost and should be more stable than naive top-k truncation. Using JSD instead of a one-sided divergence also makes sense under context mismatch, since it is more symmetric and less brittle.

Operationally, the PTD module is applied mainly to failed trajectories, converting sparse outcome rewards into token-level learning signals. The paper also claims compatibility with different RLVR optimizers, suggesting that the distillation term is modular rather than tied to one specific algorithm.

The main limitation is obvious: the quality of the hints matters a lot. If the hints are too strong, they may still leak the answer indirectly. If they are too weak, the teacher offers little additional guidance over the student and the dense supervision benefit shrinks.2d:T57b,The experiments cover LVLMs from 2B to 8B parameters on multimodal reasoning benchmarks. The headline claim is that PTD-PO consistently outperforms plain RLVR and distillation-based baselines, while maintaining higher policy entropy, improving recovery from failed rollouts, and reducing the memory overhead of online distillation.

The most important evidence to inspect is not just average benchmark accuracy, but the trade-off the method is supposed to improve:
1. whether gains are consistent across model scales;
2. whether PTD-PO beats external-teacher OPD and answer-conditioned self-distillation in the joint space of performance, training cost, and entropy preservation;
3. whether Top-K JSD with tail compensation actually saves memory and remains stable relative to full-vocabulary or naive top-k distillation;
4. whether failed-rollout recovery analyses show real credit-assignment benefits rather than generic regularization.

The abstract does not provide concrete benchmark numbers, so the main tables matter. When reading, pay special attention to which open LVLM backbones are used, how large the gains are on each benchmark, whether entropy-collapse curves are visibly delayed, and how the hint ablations behave. The hint ablations are especially important: visual cues only, textual reasoning hints only, and both together. Those details determine how convincing the method really is.2e:T433,This is a good skim paper. Start with the introduction, because the framing is the main value: pure RLVR is too sparse, external teachers are too expensive, and answer-conditioned distillation encourages shortcuts. That setup explains why the proposed method exists.

In the method section, focus on two parts. First, inspect the privileged tutoring inputs: what exactly is included in the hints, and how the paper argues they do not reveal the answer. Second, read the Top-K JSD with tail compensation objective carefully. That part determines whether the contribution is merely an engineering memory trick or a real solution to asymmetric distillation instability.

In the experiments, prioritize the main result tables, entropy curves, failed-rollout recovery analyses, and comparisons among different distillation objectives. A 25-35 minute read should be enough for the main idea. If you are considering reproduction, then the appendix becomes important, especially the hint-construction details and the memory accounting, since those are the main practical bottlenecks.2f:T554,The method has two parts. First, difficulty probing: for each reasoning step, the authors extract a step-level embedding and fit a linear regressor on a small seen dataset to map that embedding to step-level difficulty. The important distinction is that this is not initial problem difficulty; it is local difficulty as the reasoning trajectory unfolds. The authors argue that this signal is already sufficiently linear in representation space, so a simple probe is enough. Second, inference-time control: instead of hard stopping generation or training a termination head, DyCon dynamically adjusts the logits of reflection-related keywords. If estimated difficulty is low, it downweights tokens associated with further reflection, encouraging convergence. If estimated difficulty is high, it upweights those tokens, encouraging deeper exploration. This is a soft intervention rather than a hard truncation. Compared with verifier-based methods, it avoids an extra model in the loop. Compared with entropy or certainty heuristics, it does not rely on manually chosen generic thresholds. Compared with SFT/RL stopping policies, it avoids specialized supervision and the associated data dependence. The real claim is not that a linear regressor is powerful, but that evolving difficulty is already encoded in step embeddings strongly enough to drive useful control.30:T573,The evaluation spans four reasoning models from 4B to 32B and 12 benchmarks covering math reasoning, general QA, and coding. The headline result is that DyCon reduces redundant reasoning without hurting accuracy, and generalizes across models and tasks. The most important evidence is likely in three buckets. First, representation analysis: Figure 2 reportedly shows both the temporal evolution of difficulty and an approximately linear relationship between step embeddings and ground-truth step difficulty. That is the empirical foundation of the method. Second, the efficiency–performance trade-off: DyCon should reduce reasoning tokens or steps relative to standard CoT while keeping pass@1 or accuracy close; gains on easier tasks would suggest that less overthinking also reduces derailment. Third, transfer and robustness: the regressor is fit on a small seen set but is claimed to transfer to unseen benchmarks and different model scales. If that holds numerically, it suggests the probe is reading a fairly general internal signal rather than a dataset-specific artifact. The provided full-text excerpt does not include the detailed result tables, so I would not state exact deltas without checking the paper directly. When reading, verify token reduction, accuracy change, sensitivity to the chosen reflection keyword set, and how performance degrades as probe-training data shrinks.31:T900,方法核心是把每个问题的监督目标，从“固定 expert 轨迹”改成“由当前策略状态决定权重的候选轨迹池”。具体做法有三层。

第一层是构造 candidate pool。对每个问题，除了离线 expert trajectory 之外，作者还从当前模型按 on-policy rollout 采样多条解答，并通过可验证器筛出正确轨迹。这样每道题不再只有一条离线答案，而是有 expert + verified self-generated trajectories 的局部候选集合。

第二层是估计 problem-level solvability。作者用当前策略在该题上的 rollout 成功率，作为这道题对当前模型而言的“可解性”指标。这个量不是看 token 置信度，也不是看 expert 轨迹内部哪些 token 难学，而是直接问：模型自己能不能把题做出来。若成功率低，说明当前策略确实需要更强的 expert 纠偏；若成功率高，说明模型已经掌握了这类题的某些有效路径，此时继续强压单条 expert 轨迹，收益会下降，甚至会伤害已有分布。

第三层是基于 solvability 做 trajectory-level adaptive weighting，并加入 reference-constrained regularization。论文的表述里，RASFT 并不把问题显式写成 RL 的 reward/advantage 优化，而是把 expert 轨迹和模型自己生成的正确轨迹都视为“对齐候选”，然后根据 problem solvability 动态调整它们的训练权重：难题更偏向 expert，易题则放松对 expert 的刚性模仿，并允许正确的 self-rollouts 参与训练。与此同时，作者引入 frozen reference model 与当前 policy 的逆比率约束，用来抑制过度 policy drift。这个设计和 ASFT 一类 reference anchoring 有相似处，但这里的作用不是单纯稳定训练，而是更保守地“激活”模型已有推理能力，避免被新一轮 imitation 覆盖。

和已有方法相比，区别比较清楚。DFT、ASFT、ProFiT 都是在 expert trajectory 内部重加权，关注 token-level 或 objective-level 的拟合强弱；RASFT 则把适应性提升到 problem level，并显式利用 on-policy rollout 的可验证结果来决定 supervision strength。它不是简单把正确 self-generated data 混进来，而是先判断这道题当前是否还需要 expert 作为主导信号。32:T779,The method replaces the fixed expert-only target with a policy-conditioned candidate set and problem-level adaptive weighting.

First, for each problem, RASFT builds a local candidate pool containing the offline expert trajectory and verified correct trajectories sampled from the current policy. This is important: the model is not only trained on static demonstrations, but also on its own successful rollouts when they are available.

Second, it estimates a problem-level solvability score from the success rate of on-policy rollouts. This score is the central control signal. It does not ask which tokens in the expert trace are easy or hard. Instead, it asks whether the current model can already solve the problem. Low solvability means the model still needs strong expert correction; high solvability means strict imitation of one expert path is less necessary and may even be harmful.

Third, RASFT performs trajectory-level adaptive supervision. Expert trajectories receive higher influence on hard problems, while correct self-generated trajectories are allowed to contribute more on problems the model can already solve reliably. The paper frames this as policy-aware regularized SFT rather than explicit RL with rewards and advantages. In addition, it introduces an inverse-ratio constraint between a frozen reference model and the current policy to limit excessive policy drift. This reference anchoring is not only for optimization stability; it is meant to preserve the model’s existing reasoning distribution and avoid overwriting it through aggressive imitation.

Relative to prior SFT variants, the distinction is clear. DFT, ASFT, and ProFiT mainly adapt supervision within the demonstration, typically at token or objective level. RASFT moves adaptivity to the problem level and uses verified on-policy behavior to decide how much expert guidance is needed. That is the main conceptual step.33:T537,The experiments cover both math reasoning and code reasoning, comparing against standard SFT, several SFT-style variants, and GRPO.

On Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B, the reported math score improves from 25.00 to 27.72, a 10.9% relative gain. This is a meaningful result because it comes from changing the supervision scheme rather than scaling the base model or adding much more data.

On Llama-3.2-3B for code generation, performance improves from 24.93 to 31.63, a 26.9% relative gain. The larger gain on code is plausible: code tasks often have many valid solution paths and reliable verification, so over-imitation of a single reference can be especially restrictive.

Against GRPO, the paper reports an average math improvement from 20.25 to 23.47, or 15.9% relative. The right reading is not that RASFT dominates RL in general, but that when expert data and verifiers are available, a rollout-aware SFT objective can be more stable and less noisy than direct RL optimization.

The most important ablations are likely the following: removing self-generated correct trajectories, removing solvability-based weighting, removing the reference-ratio regularizer, and varying rollout count or verifier settings. Those ablations are what determine whether the gains come from genuine policy-aware supervision rather than simple data augmentation.34:T485,Start with the introduction and the method overview figure. The key distinction from DFT, ASFT, and ProFiT is that RASFT does not mainly modify token loss; it changes who the model should learn from, and by how much, on each problem.

Then read the method section carefully for four pieces: the local candidate pool, the solvability score, the trajectory-level weighting rule, and the inverse-ratio regularization with the frozen reference model. The exact training objective matters here, especially how expert and self-generated correct trajectories are mixed and normalized.

In the experiments, prioritize three things: the main comparison table against SFT-style baselines and GRPO, the ablations removing each component, and any analysis bucketed by problem difficulty or rollout success rate. If the paper includes such stratified analysis, it is the strongest evidence that the mechanism works as intended.

Estimated reading time is about 20–30 minutes for a skim. For implementation interest, pay extra attention to rollout cost, verifier assumptions, and whether the candidate-pool construction scales cleanly in larger post-training pipelines.35:T5d7,TALAN is built as a summarize-remix-writeback side path inserted into the transformer's residual stream and co-trained with LoRA or DoRA in a single SFT loop. The module first summarizes the active sequence into a compact latent memory. It then remixes that memory into token-level perturbations and writes them back into the residual stream through a controlled residual update. The design is guided by two intended properties: P1, the perturbation should remain small and bounded; P2, it should be geometrically separate from the adapter update, acting as a complementary intervention rather than duplicating what LoRA or DoRA already does.

The paper parameterizes adaptation to a new backbone through a six-axis configuration space, π = (ℓ*, T, m, w, τ, γ), covering choices such as insertion layer, summary span or window, latent memory size, writeback location or mode, mixing control, and perturbation strength. A useful design choice is that the block itself stays fixed while only the configuration changes. That makes the method easier to analyze than a highly flexible side network.

Relative to prior work, TALAN is not just another adapter. Its forward path depends on the current sequence, so different inputs induce different residual perturbations. At the same time, it avoids the multi-stage workflow of activation steering methods because there is no separate probe fitting or inference-time vector injection. Training and deployment remain close to standard SFT practice.36:T535,The experiments cover four Qwen3-family backbones and four STEM/code benchmarks, with matched LoRA and DoRA baselines. Under the LoRA pipeline, TALAN achieves a cross-model mean gain of +1.41 percentage points, is positive on all four backbones, and is non-negative on all 16 model-benchmark cells. Under the DoRA pipeline, the cross-model mean gain is +1.85 points, again positive on all four backbones, with positive deltas on 13 of 16 cells. The paper also includes matched-initialization multi-seed audits, which support positive family-level effects while explicitly showing seed variance rather than hiding it.

The added cost is small: less than 1% extra trainable parameters relative to the backbone, and only about 1.01x to 1.02x inference overhead compared with matched LoRA. As a transfer check beyond the main Qwen suite, selected Llama-3.2-1B runs are also positive across seven paired seeds under both LoRA and rsLoRA.

The analysis section focuses on two questions: whether TALAN's perturbation is orthogonal to the adapter update, and whether a small perturbation can be amplified through later layers. The reported result is that TALAN introduces a small but complementary activation change that propagates and grows with depth, which helps explain why modest writeback can still produce measurable capability gains.37:T768,The method section has two parts. First, the paper validates the mechanism. In prefix-perturbation experiments, the authors modify only the template prefix before the user query, sometimes with very small changes such as capitalization, while keeping the query semantics intact. Alignment is often restored. In contrast, if the prefix is kept unchanged, replacing the user query with similar or even random tokens can still preserve the misaligned behavior. This suggests that the trigger is concentrated in the shared prefix rather than in the semantic content of the query.

They then move from correlational evidence to a stronger causal intervention using representation patching. Specifically, they replace the KV-cache entries at prefix positions in the finetuned misaligned model with the corresponding entries from the original unfrozen? no, original frozen pre-finetuning model, across all layers. This leaves the query untouched but forces the information propagated from prefix positions to revert to its pre-finetuning state. The intervention largely restores alignment, indicating that prefix representations indeed encode the harmful behavioral bias.

Second, they introduce TReFT, Token-Regularized Finetuning. Instead of relying on retain-set interleaving, TReFT regularizes selected token representations so that shared prefix tokens do not drift too far from the base model, while allowing more of the update to land on tokens that encode the actual query content. The design choice is important: standard output supervision tells the model what behavior to produce, but not which context should scope that behavior. TReFT adds an inductive bias over attribution, pushing the model to associate new behavior with domain semantics rather than with globally shared template tokens. The paper also applies the same idea beyond EM to abstention, tool calling, and refusal behaviors.38:T44f,Three result groups matter most. First, the prefix brittleness experiments show that tiny perturbations to the chat-template prefix can sharply reduce emergent misalignment without changing the query semantics. That is the main behavioral evidence for piggybacking. Second, the KV-cache patching experiment provides the strongest causal result: on Llama-3.1-8B, patching prefix representations from the unfinetuned model raises the alignment score from 40.8 to 90.4. This is a large recovery and suggests the issue is encoded in internal prefix representations rather than only in output-layer drift. Third, TReFT consistently mitigates OOD misgeneralization while preserving the intended in-domain behavior. The headline number in the paper is that on Llama-3.1-7B in the legal-domain setting, TReFT reduces EM 33.5% better than data interleaving. In broader narrow-behavior finetuning settings, it reduces unintended generalization by 54.3% relative to naive SFT. The utility-preservation result is also important, because many mitigation methods work by simply weakening the learned behavior overall.39:T99e,There is no new model or training recipe here, but the argument is structured carefully. The paper starts by criticizing the “fix it in post” mindset: when undesirable behavior appears, the default response is finetuning, RLHF, or input/output filtering, rather than asking how the behavior was produced during pretraining or intermediate training. The authors use examples such as brittle safety finetuning and superficial bias mitigation to argue that post-hoc interventions often change outputs without addressing the underlying causal mechanism.

The paper then borrows from Kuhn’s criteria for scientific theories and asks what a science of AI should look like. The answer is not just better benchmark optimization. A scientific account should support empirical prediction, internal consistency across optimization/data/architecture/representation changes, compatibility with adjacent theory, broad scope beyond one benchmark, simplicity where possible, and the ability to generate new experiments.

The most actionable part is the three-level agenda around training dynamics. First is prediction: using early training signals to forecast later capabilities, failure modes, and alignment properties, not only final loss. This points toward studying gradient statistics, representation geometry, sample learning order, subdistribution absorption rates, and the timing of capability emergence. Second is intervention: if a trajectory is going wrong, one should be able to steer it through data mixture changes, sampling schedules, optimizer settings, regularization, or stage transitions. Third is design: replacing large amounts of trial-and-error with training procedures that are intentionally constructed to yield target properties.

Methodologically, the paper argues for longitudinal measurement rather than endpoint-only evaluation. That means linking checkpoints, internal states, behavior, and exposure history across training time. This stands in contrast to much of mechanistic interpretability, which often studies final models, and much of alignment work, which often studies post-trained behavior without tracing its developmental origin. The paper implicitly calls for stronger experimental design as well: controlled data-mixture studies, repeated runs to estimate trajectory variance, sample-level learning event logging, and tests of whether post-training actually changes internal representations rather than only decision boundaries.3a:T464,The technical strategy is a unified framework supported by mathematical appendices and instantiated across multiple modalities. The text appears to start from intelligence, memory, prediction, and compression, then builds the machinery through optimization, automatic differentiation, entropy, diffusion, denoising, and rate-distortion theory. It then applies the framework to image autoencoding and sampling, text-conditioned image generation, video extension, 3D tokenization and generation, motion generation, natural language modeling, and white-box transformer variants. For language, the key implementation seems to be a model called Causal CRATE, together with sections on optimization strategy, evaluation methodology, width scaling, linear-time transformers, and attention-only variants. The novelty is not a single algorithmic trick. It is the attempt to argue that representation learning, generative modeling, and memory organization share a common mathematical structure, and that diffusion and denoising are not image-specific tools but general mechanisms for information recovery and representation evolution.3b:T4cc,This is best approached selectively. If your focus is LLM pretraining, start with Section 8.11 on natural language representation and generation, and inspect the objective, the Causal CRATE architecture, the optimization strategy, and the evaluation setup. Then read Section 8.12 on scaling and improving white-box transformers, especially the discussions of width scaling, linear-time complexity, and attention-only variants, to judge whether the conclusions transfer to modern large-scale language models. If you care about alternative language-modeling paradigms, pair those chapters with Appendix B, which develops the links among entropy, diffusion, denoising, and lossy coding; that part can help build a cleaner conceptual bridge to diffusion LMs, masked LMs, and latent modeling. A practical reading order is: Chapter 1 for the framing, then 8.11, then 8.12, then Appendix A on optimization, and finally Appendix B. A skim of the relevant parts may take a few hours, while a serious read will take much longer. The key is to translate the abstract framework back into concrete pretraining questions such as data repetition, representation compression, tokenizer granularity, corruption design, and latent-space sampling.3c:T434,Dataset: ultra-high-field 7T fMRI, 8 English speakers, 200 diverse sentences, each repeated 3 times; GLM yields one beta per voxel per sentence (averaged across repeats). Voxels are filtered by reliability (noise ceiling SNR > 0.4) and restricted to the left hemisphere.
Voxel groupings:
- PC-based populations from prior work: PC1 (processing difficulty; tied to frequency/surprisal) and PC2 (concrete–abstract content), yielding Hard/Easy/Abstract/Concrete/Ghost voxel sets.
- Language-network fROIs (Fedorenko-style): IFG, IFGorb, MFG, AntTemp, PostTemp.

Main reported findings (as described in the provided full text): SAE features match dense residual-stream features in encoding accuracy while enabling feature-level interpretations; frontal regions are more explained by difficulty (surprisal), whereas temporal regions rely more on LM-derived content features; and alignment is primarily supported by more general SAE features rather than idiosyncratic ones.

Exact metrics and numeric deltas are likely in the Results/Appendix sections not included in the excerpt.3d:T43c,Pipeline: empirical symptom → interpretability tools → subspace identification → linear filtering.
1) Symptom via Logit Lens: projecting raw text embeddings through the unembedding matrix yields top vocabulary probabilities dominated by high-frequency tokens across model families.
2) Hypothesis: embedding anisotropy concentrates representations around a frequency-weighted centroid (“average token”), suppressing semantic variation.
3) Subspace discovery via Logit Spectroscopy: analyzing a reverse-engineered average-token representation reveals an “edge spectrum” subspace spanned by right singular vectors at both ends of the unembedding matrix’s singular value spectrum (largest and smallest singular values).
4) Fix: EmbedFilter, a simple linear transformation that truncates/removes embedding components in this edge-spectrum subspace. It is constructed from existing unembedding parameters and requires no training.
5) Claimed side benefit: approximately distance-preserving behavior enables dimensionality reduction for cheaper indexing and faster retrieval.3e:T803,The analysis uses a standard tractable testbed for ICL theory: a single-layer linear attention model trained on linear regression prompts. This choice is deliberate. Full transformers are too hard to analyze cleanly, while linear attention has been shown in prior work to reproduce several qualitative ICL phenomena. The paper’s main modeling contribution is the task distribution: instead of sampling task vectors from a single family, it samples them from a mixture of Gaussians, with each mixture component restricted to a low-dimensional subspace. These subspaces overlap through a shared common subspace.

This structure lets the authors study how gradient descent first aligns with the shared subspace and only later captures component-specific directions. The loss plateau is interpreted as the phase before the model has identified the common structure that is useful across tasks. Once that shared structure is learned, gradients from different task components reinforce each other, and optimization accelerates. In that sense, diversity helps not because tasks are arbitrarily different, but because multiple related tasks provide a stronger and more stable signal on the common latent structure.

For generalization, especially OOD, the paper argues that success depends on whether test tasks lie near the span or coverage induced by the training subspaces. If training tasks cover a richer set of structured directions while still sharing common components, the model can extrapolate better. If test tasks fall outside that structural support, diversity alone does not guarantee transfer. This is a useful distinction for pretraining: coverage matters, but structured coverage matters more.

Relative to earlier work, Kim et al. mainly provided empirical evidence that task diversity shortens the plateau, and Goddard et al. empirically linked diversity to OOD behavior under a different task definition. This paper’s novelty is the overlapping-subspace formulation that makes both effects analyzable within one mathematical framework.3f:T4bb,The experiments serve mainly to validate the theory and reproduce known qualitative phenomena rather than to establish a new benchmark. The paper reproduces the observation that training on a mixture of related task families converges faster than training on each family separately; the left panel of Figure 2 is highlighted as showing earlier escape from the plateau and faster approach to near-optimal performance under task diversity. It also varies the degree of subspace overlap, the number of mixture components, and the dimensions of shared versus private subspaces to show how these factors affect plateau length and OOD behavior.

The experimental setup stays within the linear-regression / linear-attention testbed. The main outputs are trends, not headline absolute numbers: stronger shared subspaces lead to larger optimization gains from joint training, and broader structured subspace coverage improves OOD generalization. The provided full-content excerpt does not include the complete result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact gains.
